### Education

The literacy rate in 2011 was 74.04%: 65.46% among females and 82.14% among males. The rural-urban literacy gap, which was 21.2 percentage points in 2001, dropped to 16.1 percentage points in 2011. The improvement in the rural literacy rate is twice that of urban areas. In 2011 [Kerala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kerala) had the highest literary rate, with 93.91% literacy, and [Bihar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bihar) the lowest, with 63.82%. In 1981 the respective literacy rates for total population, men and women were 41%, 53% and 29%. In 1951, the rates were 18%, 27% and 9%. In 1921, the rates 7%, 12% and 2%. In 1891, they were 5%, 9% and 1%. According to Latika Chaudhary, in 1911 there were under three primary schools for every ten villages. Statistically, more caste and religious diversity reduced private spending. Primary schools taught literacy, so local diversity limited its growth.

The education system of India is the world's second-largest. India has over 900 universities, 40,000 colleges and 1.5 million schools. In India's higher education system, a significant number of seats are reserved under [affirmative action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reservation_in_India) policies for the historically disadvantaged. In recent decades India's improved education system is often cited as one of the main contributors to [its economic development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_development_in_India).

It was foundedin 1847 during [East India Company rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Company_rule_in_India) to train men employed in the construction of the [Ganges Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganges_Canal).

### Health

India bears a disproportionately large burden of the world's [tuberculosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuberculosis_in_India) rates, with [World Health Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Health_Organization) (WHO) statistics for 2022 estimating 2.8 million new infections annually, accounting for 26% of the global total. It is estimated that approximately 40% of the population of India carry [tuberculosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latent_tuberculosis) infection.

In 2018 [chronic obstructive pulmonary disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chronic_obstructive_pulmonary_disease) was the leading cause of death after [heart disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heart_disease_in_India). The 10 [most polluted cities in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_most-polluted_cities_by_particulate_matter_concentration) are all in India with more than 140 million people breathing air 10 times or more over the WHO safe limit. As of 2026, air quality reports show that not a single Indian city meets the WHO's safety guidelines. In cities like Delhi and Mumbai, breathing the air is equivalent to smoking 30 cigarettes a day. In 2017, air pollution killed 1.24 million Indians.

## Culture

### Society

Although sometimes applied to other cultures and religions, caste is a uniquely Indian, and Hindu, social institution. All Hindus fall broadly into four castes, or *[varnas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varna_%28Hinduism%29)*: [Brahmin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahmin), or priests, at the top; below them [Kshatriya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kshatriya), or warriors; further below, [Vaishya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaishya), or merchants and farmers; and at the bottom, [Shudra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shudra), or the service class. Outside the caste system, and therefore of traditional Hinduism, lie people formerly called "outcastes" or "untouchables," and now [scheduled caste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scheduled_caste) (a term used in India's constitution) or [Dalit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dalit), a later self-description of pride, meaning "broken" or "downtrodden". Each caste is further divided into sub-castes, or [jatis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C4%81ti), many of which are tied to occupations. The custom of [endogamy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endogamy), or marrying within one's subcaste, however, makes caste a hereditary label, not of one occupational choice, and has caused the caste system, therefore, to become entrenched. The [Constituent Assembly of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constituent_Assembly_of_India) abolished untouchability in 1947, the [Republic of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_India) did more formally in 1950, and India has since enacted other anti-discriminatory laws and social welfare initiatives related to caste. Still, caste-based inequality, discrimination, segregation, and [violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caste-related_violence_in_India) persist.

Multi-generational [patrilineal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrilineal) [joint families](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_family) have been the norm in India, though [nuclear families](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_family) are becoming common in urban areas. A very large majority of Indians have [their marriages arranged](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arranged_marriage_in_the_Indian_subcontinent) by their parents or family elders. Marriage is thought to be for life; and the divorce rate is extremely low; less than one in a thousand marriages end in divorce. Many women marry before reaching 18, which is their legal marriageable age; [child marriages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_marriage) are not uncommon, especially in rural areas. In large parts of Hindu northern India, moreover, a form of territorial [exogamy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exogamy) is observed in which a bride marries out of her natal village and her parents do not visit her in her married home; the annual rite [raksha bandhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raksha_bandhan), during which married women return to their [natal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birth) homes, has served both to affirm bonds with their natal families and offer a recourse in times of marital stress.

### Visual art

India has a very ancient tradition of art, which has exchanged many influences with the rest of [Eurasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasia), especially in the first millennium. During this period [Buddhist art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_art) spread with Indian religions to [Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Asia), [East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Asia) and [Southeast Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Asia), the last also greatly influenced by Hindu art. Thousands of [seals from the Indus Valley civilisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_Valley_civilisation#Seals) of the third millennium BCE have been found, usually carved with animals, but also some with human figures. The [Pashupati seal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashupati_seal), excavated in [Mohenjo-daro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohenjo-daro), Pakistan, in 1928–29, is the best known. Virtually no art survives from a long period following the Indus Valley Civilisation. Almost all surviving ancient Indian art thereafter is in various forms of religious [sculpture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_sculpture) in durable materials, or coins. There was probably originally far more in wood, which is lost. In north India [Mauryan art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mauryan_art) is the first imperial movement.

Over the following centuries a distinctly Indian style of sculpting the human figure developed, with less interest in articulating precise anatomy than [ancient Greek sculpture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_sculpture) but showing smoothly flowing forms expressing *prana* ("breath" or life-force). This is often complicated by the need to give figures multiple arms or heads, or represent different genders on the left and right of figures, as with the [Ardhanarishvara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ardhanarishvara) form of Shiva and [Parvati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parvati).

Most of the earliest large sculpture is Buddhist, either excavated from Buddhist [stupas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stupa) such as [Sanchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanchi), [Sarnath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarnath) and [Amaravati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amaravati_Stupa), or is rock cut [reliefs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relief) at sites such as [Ajanta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ajanta_Caves), [Karla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karla_Caves) and [Ellora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ellora). Hindu and Jain sites appear rather later. In spite of this complex mixture of religious traditions, generally, the prevailing artistic style at any time and place has been shared by the major religious groups, and sculptors probably usually served all communities. [Gupta art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gupta_art), at its peak c. 300 CE – c. 500 CE, is often regarded as a classical period whose influence lingered for many centuries after; it saw a new dominance of Hindu sculpture, as at the [Elephanta Caves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elephanta_Caves). Across the north, this became rather stiff and formulaic after c. 800 CE, though rich with finely carved detail in the surrounds of statues. But in the South, under the [Pallava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pallava_dynasty) and [Chola dynasties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chola_dynasty), sculpture in both stone and bronze had a [sustained period of great achievement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chola_art_and_architecture#Sculpture_and_bronzes); the large bronzes with Shiva as [Nataraja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nataraja) have become an iconic symbol of India.

Ancient paintings have only survived at a few sites, of which the crowded scenes of court life in the [Ajanta Caves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ajanta_Caves) are some of the most important. Painted manuscripts of religious texts survive from Eastern India from 10th century onwards, most of the earliest being Buddhist and later Jain. These significantly influenced later artistic styles. The Persian-derived [Deccan painting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deccan_painting), starting just before the [Mughal miniature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_miniature), between them give the first large body of secular painting, with an emphasis on portraits, and the recording of princely pleasures and wars. The style spread to Hindu courts, especially [among the Rajputs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajput_painting), and developed a variety of styles, with the smaller courts often the most innovative, with figures such as [Nihâl Chand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nih%C3%A2l_Chand) and [Nainsukh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nainsukh). As a market developed among European residents, it was supplied by [Company painting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Company_painting) by Indian artists with considerable Western influence. In the 19th century, cheap [Kalighat paintings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalighat_painting) of gods and everyday life, done on paper, were urban [folk art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_art) from [Calcutta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcutta), which later saw the [Bengal School of Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal_School_of_Art), reflecting the art colleges founded by the British, the first movement in [modern Indian painting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Indian_painting).

### Mathematics

Significant mathematics began in India in the first millennium BCE. The *[Śulba Sūtras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shulba_Sutras)* (literally, "Aphorisms of the Chords" in [Vedic Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vedic_Sanskrit)) (c. 700–400 BCE) contain the earliest extant verbal expression of the [Pythagorean theorem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pythagorean_theorem) (although very likely it had been known to the [Old Babylonians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Babylonian_dynasty).) All mathematical works were orally transmitted until approximately 500 BCE; thereafter, they were transmitted both orally and in manuscript form. The oldest extant mathematical document produced on the Indian subcontinent is the birch bark [Bakhshali manuscript](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bakhshali_manuscript) from the 7th century CE.

In the classical period of Indian mathematics (400 CE to 1200 CE), important contributions were made by [Aryabhata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aryabhata), [Brahmagupta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahmagupta), [Bhaskara II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhaskara_II), [Varāhamihira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Var%C4%81hamihira), and [Madhava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madhava_of_Sangamagrama). The [decimal number system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decimal) in use today was first recorded in Indian mathematics. Indian mathematicians made early contributions to the study of the concept of [zero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/0_%28number%29) as a number, [negative numbers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negative_numbers), [arithmetic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arithmetic), and [algebra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algebra). [Trigonometry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trigonometry) was further advanced in India, and the modern definitions of [sine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sine) and [cosine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosine) were developed there. These mathematical concepts were transmitted to the Middle East, China, and Europe. A later landmark in Indian mathematics was the development of the [series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Series_%28mathematics%29) expansions for [trigonometric functions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trigonometric_function) (sine, cosine, and [arc tangent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arc_tangent)) by mathematicians of the [Kerala school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kerala_school_of_astronomy_and_mathematics) in the 15th century CE. Their work, completed two centuries before the invention of [calculus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calculus) in Europe, provided the first example of a [power series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_series). In the modern era [Srinivasa Ramanujan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srinivasa_Ramanujan) made fundamental contributions to [number theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Number_theory).

### Music

India contains a wide array of musical practices, including many different [folk musics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_folk_music) from different regions. [Indian classical music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_classical_music) has Vedic origins, and split in the 13th century into the two main traditions of [Hindustani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindustani_classical_music) and [Carnatic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnatic_music) music. Hindustani is associated with North India and is more [improvisational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musical_improvisation), featuring instruments such as the [sitar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sitar) and [tabla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabla), and Carnatic is South Indian and more focused on written [compositions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musical_composition) such as the *[kriti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kriti_%28music%29)*, while both styles contain common elements such as the [raga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raga) melodic framework and [tala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tala_%28music%29) rhythmic meter. Indian music has influenced western genres, including [rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_music) and [jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz) musicians during the [1960s counterculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counterculture_of_the_1960s).

[Indian film music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_film_music) is music written for [Indian cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_India), generally composed by [music directors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Indian_film_music_directors) and sung by [playback singers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Playback_singers). Modern [Indian pop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_pop) takes influences from classical, folk, and western pop music.

### Dance

Dance in India has drawn heavily from [Indian classical dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_classical_dance) traditions. Many of these in turn arose in temples or other religious contexts. Their sponsorship and promotion, however, has continued in secular, modern India. India also has local and modern dance traditions. Whether a dance is classical is determined by the [Sangeet Natak Academi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sangeet_Natak_Academi), the Indian government's organisation for performing arts. Although more dances could perhaps meet the criteria for classical, the Akademi has chosen eight.

The best-known classical dance is Bharatnatyam, which began in the temple dances of [Tamil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamil_people) [devadasis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devadasi). Identified with "prostitutes and courtesans", their dancing was formally banned in 1947. Concurrently, the dance was rehabilitated as a "pure" art form, with [Rukmini Devi Arundale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rukmini_Devi_Arundale) as a prominent figure. A devdasi who went on to attain national and international prominence was [Thanjavur Balasaraswati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balasaraswati). Some sources consider the dance-dramas [Chhau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chhau_dance) of [Jharkhand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jharkhand), [West Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Bengal), and [Odisha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odisha) and [Yakshagana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yakshagana) of [Karnataka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karnataka) to also belong to the classical tradition.

Local dance traditions vary widely across India. In addition to the dance-dramas Chhau and Yakshagana, they include dance-dramas [Raslila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raslila) of western [Uttar Pradesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uttar_Pradesh) and [Terukkuttu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terukkuttu) of Tamil Nadu; calendrical and festival dances such as the [Bhangra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhangra_%28dance%29) of Punjab, especially at [Vaisakhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaisakhi), the onset of spring, and [Garba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garba_%28dance%29) of [Gujarat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gujarat) during [Navratri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navratri); and tribal or [Adivasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adivasi) dances, such as those of the [Santal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santal) and [Toda people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toda_people), the latter, for example, in honour of the god [Ön](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toda_people#Religion) who brought buffalo to earth.

Among 20th-century directions is the [modern dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_dance) of [Uday Shankar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uday_Shankar) in which classical styles were employed but not adhered to rigidly. Examples are dance-dramas based on the ancient Indian [animal fables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_fable), *[Panchatantra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panchatantra)*, and Nehru's mid-century meditation on Indian history, *[The Discovery of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Discovery_of_India)*. Dance has been an essential aspect of Indian films from the first [talkies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talkies) of the 1930s. The individual and group dances of [Bollywood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bollywood), for example, show a broad range of influences, including classical, local, and Western popular dance. Towards the end of the 20th century, innovations in [British South Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_South_Asian) music and dance, such as [Post-Bhangra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_the_United_Kingdom_%281990s%29#Post-Bhangra), fed back into dance in India.

### Clothing

From ancient times until the advent of the modern, the most widely worn traditional dress in India was [draped](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Draped_garment). For women it took the form of a [sari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sari), a single piece of cloth many yards long. The sari was traditionally wrapped around the lower body and the shoulder. In its modern form, it is combined with an underskirt, or Indian [petticoat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petticoat#Asian_petticoats), and tucked in along the waist band for more secure fastening. It is also commonly worn with an Indian [blouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blouse), or [choli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Choli), which serves as the primary upper-body garment, the sari's end—passing over the shoulder—covering the midriff and obscuring the upper body's contours. For men, a similar but shorter length of cloth, the [dhoti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhoti), has served as a lower-body garment.

The use of stitched clothes became widespread after Muslim rule was established by the [Delhi sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi_sultanate) (c. 1300 CE) and continued by the [Mughal Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire) (c. 1525 CE). Among the garments introduced during this time and still commonly worn are: the [shalwars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shalwar_kameez) and [pyjamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyjama), both styles of trousers, and the tunics [kurta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurta) and [kameez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kameez). Shalwars are atypically wide at the waist but narrow to a cuffed bottom. They are held up by a drawstring, which causes them to become pleated around the waist. When the pants are cut quite narrow, on the [bias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grain_%28textile%29#Bias), they are called [churidars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Churidar). The kameez is a long shirt or tunic. Its side seams left open below the waistline. The kurta is traditionally collarless and made of cotton or silk; it is worn plain or with embroidered decoration, such as [chikankari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chikankari); and typically falls to either just above or just below the wearer's knees.

In the last 50 years, fashions have changed a great deal in India. Increasingly, in urban northern India, the sari is no longer the apparel of everyday wear, though they remain popular on formal occasions. The traditional shalwar kameez is rarely worn by younger urban women, who favour churidars or jeans. In office settings, ubiquitous air conditioning allows men to wear sports jackets year-round. For weddings and formal occasions, men in the middle and upper classes often wear bandhgala, or short [Nehru jackets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nehru_jackets), with pants, with the groom and his [groomsmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Groomsmen) sporting [sherwanis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sherwani) and churidars.

### Cuisine

The foundation of a typical Indian meal is a cereal cooked plainly and complemented with savoury dishes. The cooked cereal could be steamed rice; [chapati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chapati), a thin unleavened bread; [idli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idli), a steamed breakfast cake; or [dosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dosa_%28food%29), a griddled pancake. The savoury dishes might include [lentils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lentil), [pulses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulses), vegetables, meat, poultry and fish commonly spiced with [ginger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ginger_root) and [garlic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garlic), but also [coriander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coriander), [cumin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumin), [turmeric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turmeric), [cinnamon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinnamon), [cardamom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardamom) and others. In some instances, the ingredients may be mixed during the cooking process. India has distinctive vegetarian cuisines, each a feature of the geographical and cultural histories of its adherents. About 20% to 39% of India's population consists of vegetarians. Although meat is eaten widely, the proportional consumption of meat is low.

The most significant import of cooking techniques into India during the last millennium occurred during the [Mughal Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire), spreading into northern India from regions to its northwest, along with dishes such as [pilaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pilaf). Onions, garlic, almonds, and spices were added to the simple yogurt marinade of Persia. Rice was partially cooked and layered alternately with sauteed meat, the pot sealed tightly, and slow cooked according to another Persian cooking technique, to produce [biryani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biryani), a feature of festive dining in many parts of India.

The diversity of Indian food served worldwide has been partially concealed by the dominance of [Punjabi cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_cuisine). The popularity of [tandoori chicken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tandoori_chicken)—cooked in a [tandoor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tandoor) oven, which had traditionally been used for baking bread in the rural Punjab and the Delhi region, especially among Muslims, but which is originally from [Central Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Asia)—dates to the 1950s, and was caused in large part by an entrepreneurial response among people from the Punjab who had been displaced by the 1947 partition.

### Sports

Several traditional indigenous sports such as *[kabaddi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabaddi)*, *[kho kho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kho_kho)*, *[pehlwani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pehlwani)* and *[gilli-danda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilli-danda)*, and also [martial arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_martial_arts), such as *[Kalarippayattu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalarippayattu)* and *[marma adi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marma_adi)* remain popular. [Chess](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chess) is commonly held to have [originated in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_chess#India) as *[chaturaṅga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaturanga)*; There has been a rise in the number of Indian [grandmasters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grandmaster_%28chess%29). [Viswanathan Anand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viswanathan_Anand) became the undisputed [Chess World Champion in 2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Chess_Championship_2007) and held the status until 2013. [Parcheesi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parcheesi) is derived from *[Pachisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pachisi)* another traditional Indian pastime, which in early modern times was played on a giant marble court by [Mughal emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire) [Akbar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akbar).

[Cricket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cricket_in_India) is the most popular sport in India. [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India_national_cricket_team) is one of the more successful cricket teams, having won two [Cricket World Cups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cricket_World_Cup), three [T20 World Cups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Men's_T20_World_Cup), and three [Champions Trophies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ICC_Champions_Trophy). [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India_men's_national_field_hockey_team) has won a record eight [field hockey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Field_hockey) gold medals in the [summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Field_hockey_at_the_Summer_Olympics).

- [The Indian hockey team, captained by Dhyan Chand (standing second from l.), after winning the finals at the 1936 Summer Olympics – their third of six consecutive Olympic golds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Indian-Hockey-Team-Berlin-1936.jpg)The Indian hockey team, captained by [Dhyan Chand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhyan_Chand) (standing second from l.), after winning the finals at the [1936 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1936_Summer_Olympics) – their third of six consecutive Olympic golds

"[...] *Jana Gana Mana* is the National Anthem of India, subject to such alterations in the words as the Government may authorise as occasion arises; and the song *Vande Mataram*, which has played a historic part in the struggle for Indian freedom, shall be honoured equally with *Jana Gana Mana* and shall have equal status with it."
1. According to [Part XVII of the Constitution of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Part_XVII_of_the_Constitution_of_India), [Hindi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_Hindi) in the [Devanagari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devanagari) script is the [official language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_language) of the Union, along with [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_English) as an additional official language. [States and union territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/States_and_union_territories_of_India) can have a different official language of their own other than Hindi or English.
1. Not all the state-level official languages are in the eighth schedule and not all the scheduled languages are state-level official languages. For example, the [Sindhi language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sindhi_language) is an 8th scheduled but not a state-level official language.
1. [Kashmiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmiri_language) and [Dogri language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dogri_language) are the official languages of [Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_and_Kashmir_%28union_territory%29) which is currently a [union territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_territory) and no longer the [former state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_and_Kashmir_%28state%29).
 - According to [Ethnologue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnologue), there are 426 living indigenous languages in India, in contrast to 11 extinct indigenous languages. In addition, there are 32 living non-indigenous languages.
 - Different sources give widely differing figures, primarily based on how the terms "language" and "dialect" are defined and grouped.

1. the historic religion of the [Vedas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vedas), and the precursor to [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism)
1. The Mauryan economy was helped by the rise of Buddhism and Jainism, creeds that promoted nonviolence, proscribed ostentation, or superfluous sacrifices and rituals, and reduced the costs of economic transactions; by coinage that increased economic accommodation in the region; and by the use of writing, which might have boosted more intricate business dealings.
1. To promote movement and trade, the Mauryans built roads, most prominently a chiefly winter-time road—the Uttarapath—which connected eastern Afghanistan to their capital [Pataliputra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patliputra), during the time of year when the water levels in the intersecting rivers were low, and they could be easily [forded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ford_%28crossing%29).
1. When [Harsha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harsha) of [Kannauj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kannauj), who ruled much of the Indo-Gangetic Plain from 606 to 647 CE, attempted to expand southwards, he was defeated by the [Chalukya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chalukya_dynasty) ruler of the Deccan. When his successor attempted to expand eastwards, he was defeated by the [Pala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pala_Empire) king of [Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal). When the Chalukyas attempted to expand southwards, they were defeated by the [Pallavas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pallava_dynasty) from farther south, who in turn were opposed by the [Pandyas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pandya_dynasty) and the [Cholas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chola_dynasty) from still farther south.
1. Central Asian warriors became supreme during South Asia's medieval transition by deploying swift-horse cavalry skilled in firing arrows at full gallop, volley after volley.
1. The northernmost point under Indian control is the disputed [Siachen Glacier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siachen_Glacier) in [Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_and_Kashmir_%28union_territory%29); however, the [Government of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_India) regards the entire region of the former princely state of Jammu and Kashmir, including [Gilgit-Baltistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilgit-Baltistan) administered by Pakistan, to be its territory. It therefore assigns the latitude 37° 6′ to its northernmost point.
1. A biodiversity hotspot is a [biogeographical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biogeography) region which has more than 1,500 [vascular plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vascular_plant) species, but less than 30% of its primary habitat.
1. During the last years of [British rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj), elections were held in 1946 in all [provinces of British India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidencies_and_provinces_of_British_India). The Congress won electoral victories in eight of 11 provinces. A Congress-led government was formed in September; Jawaharlal Nehru served as interim prime minister.
1. Congress had the support of [left-leaning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Left-wing_politics) parties and MPs who opposed the BJP.
1. The 0% results from rounding to two decimal places.
1. Caste is a form of [social stratification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_stratification) characterised by [endogamy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endogamy), hereditary transmission of an occupation, ritual status in a hierarchy, and customary social interaction or exclusion defined by cultural notions of purity and pollution.
1. Ancient and medieval Indian mathematical works, all composed in [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit) usually consisted of two sections: *[sutras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sutra)* in which a set of rules or problems were stated with economy in verse, and a prose commentary that explained the problem in more detail and provided justification for the solution.
1. Apart from geometric series
1. Among young urban middle-class women, for example, a proficiency in classical dance is sometimes a sought-after social achievement.
1. The classical status increases a dance's visibility and attracts more funding from agencies and ticket purchases from audiences.
1. Given the geographical distribution of the chosen dances and their stylistic range, the choices could be seen as a facet of India's ethos of national integration.
1. As per the [Madras Devadasis (Prevention of Dedication) Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madras_Devadasis_%28Prevention_of_Dedication%29_Act) after agitation from the Indian middle and upper classes.
1. The [Central Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Asia) custom of buying bread outside the home accompanied the [Mughals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire) to India.
1. Genetic studies have shown that mangos were first domesticated in the region between northeastern India, northwestern Myanmar, and Bangladesh.

One of these is the idea of India as 'the world's largest democracy', but a democracy forged less by the creation of representative institutions and expanding electorate under British rule than by the endeavours of India's founding fathers – Gandhi, Nehru, Patel and Ambedkar – and the labours of the Constituent Assembly between 1946 and 1949, embodied in the Indian constitution of 1950. This democratic order, reinforced by the regular holding of nationwide elections and polling for the state assemblies, has, it can be argued, consistently underpinned a fundamentally democratic state structure – despite the anomaly of the Emergency and the apparent durability of the Gandhi-Nehru dynasty.

Eventually, various bands entered India between 75,000 years ago and 35,000 years ago."

1. [Lowe 2015](#CITEREFLowe2015), pp. 1–2: "[It](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rigveda) consists of 1,028 hymns (*sūktas*), highly crafted poetic compositions originally intended for recital during rituals and for the invocation of and communication with the Indo-Aryan gods. Modern scholarly opinion largely agrees that these hymns were composed between around 1500 BCE and 1200 BCE, during the eastward migration of the Indo-Aryan tribes from the mountains of what is today northern Afghanistan across the Punjab into north India."
 - [Witzel 2003](#CITEREFWitzel2003), pp. 68–70: "It is known from internal evidence that the Vedic texts were orally composed in northern India, at first in the Greater Punjab and later on also in more eastern areas, including northern Bihar, between ca. 1500 BCE and ca. 500–400 BCE. The oldest text, the Rgveda, must have been more or less contemporary with the Mitanni texts of northern Syria/Iraq (1450–1350 BCE); [...] The Vedic texts were orally composed and transmitted, without the use of script, in an unbroken line of transmission from teacher to student that was formalised early on. This ensured an impeccable textual transmission superior to the classical texts of other cultures; it is in fact something of a *tape-recording* of ca. 1500–500 BCE. Not just the actual words, but even the long-lost musical (tonal) accent (as in old Greek or in Japanese) has been preserved up to the present. [...] The RV text was composed before the introduction and massive use of iron, that is before ca. 1200–1000 BCE."
 - [Doniger 2014](#CITEREFDoniger2014), pp. xviii, 10: "A Chronology of Hinduism: ca. 1500–1000 BCE Rig Veda; ca. 1200–900 BCE Yajur Veda, Sama Veda and Atharva Veda [...] Hindu texts began with the *Rig Veda* ('Knowledge of Verses'), composed in northwest India around 1500 BCE; the first of the three Vedas, it is the earliest extant text composed in Sanskrit, the language of ancient India."

[...] Although Vedic religion is very different in many regards from what is known as Classical Hinduism, the seeds are there. Gods like Viṣṇu and Śiva (under the name Rudra), who will become so dominant later, are already present in the Ṛgveda, though in roles both lesser than and different from those they will later play, and the principal Ṛgvedic gods like Indra remain in later Hinduism, though in diminished capacity."
 - [Flood 2020](#CITEREFFlood2020), p. 4, see note 4: "I take the term 'Hinduism' to meaningfully denote a range and history of practice characterised by a number of features, particularly reference to Vedic textual and sacrificial origins, belonging to endogamous social units (*jāti*/*varṇa*), participating in practices that involve making an offering to a deity and receiving a blessing (*pūjā*), and a first-level cultural polytheism (although many Hindus adhere to a second-level monotheism in which many gods are regarded as emanations or manifestations of the one, supreme being)."
 - [Michaels 2017](#CITEREFMichaels2017), p. 86: "Almost all traditional Hindu families observe until today at least three *samskaras* (initiation, marriage, and death ritual). Most other rituals have lost their popularity, are combined with other rites of passage, or are drastically shortened. Although *samskaras* vary from region to region, from class (*varna*) to class, and from caste to caste, their core elements remain the same owing to the common source, the Veda, and a common priestly tradition preserved by the *Brahmin* priests."
 - [Flood 1996](#CITEREFFlood1996), p. 35: "It is this Sanskrit, vedic, tradition which has maintained a continuity into modern times and which has provided the most important resource and inspiration for Hindu traditions and individuals. The Veda is the foundation for most later developments in what is known as Hinduism."

The actual term Hindu first occurs as a Persian geographical term for the people who lived beyond the Indus River. The term Hindu originated as a geographical term and did not refer to a religion. Later, Hindu was taken by European languages from the Arabic term al-Hind, which referred to the people who lived across the Indus River. This Arabic term was itself taken from the Persian term Hindū, which refers to all Indians. By the 13th century, Hindustan emerged as a popular alternative name for India, meaning the "land of Hindus."

At the strategic choke point, just before reaching the fertile, well-watered Gangetic plain, sat Delhi. In Choudhry, Sujit; Khosla, Madhav; Mehta, Pratap Bhanu (eds.). *The Oxford Handbook of the Indian Constitution*. Oxford University Press. pp. 63–64. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_%28identifier%29) [978-0-19-870489-8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-19-870489-8). Ultimately, it was the pragmatic consensus-seeking approach that triumphed. On 14 September 1949, after three years of debate, the assembly overwhelmingly approved a compromise resolution, known as the Munshi—Ayyangar formula, which later became Articles 343-51 of the Indian Constitution. Instead of declaring a 'national language', Hindi was labelled the 'official language of the Union', while English was to continue to be used 'for all official purposes'. It was decided that this arrangement would apply for a period of fifteen years, during which time Hindi was to be progressively introduced into official use. What would happen at the end of this interim period was left undetermined, with the Constitution providing for the establishment of a parliamentary committee to examine the issue in the future. In addition, the Constitution recognised fourteen other languages for official use (listed in the Eighth Schedule of the Constitution).... Fifteen years after the enactment of the Constitution, Hindi was still not widely used by the Union government. Following a series of violent riots in non-Hindi-speaking States in the 1960s, Parliament renounced the ideal of an Indian national language. In 1965, when the fifteen-year interim period prescribed by the Constitution elapsed, the government announced that English would remain the de facto formal language of India.
1. Isaka, Riho (2021). [*Language, Identity, and Power in Modern India: Gujarat, c. 1850–1960*](https://books.google.com/books?id=3F5CEAAAQBAJ&pg=PA127). Routledge New Horizons in South Asian Studies. Routledge. pp. 126–197. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_%28identifier%29) [978-1-000-46858-8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-1-000-46858-8). Partition may have 'killed' Hindustani, but it had a marked effect on the debates regarding the position of English and provincial languages in the Constitution. The Hindi protagonists became even more insistent on establishing Hindi as the sole national language and imposing it on the non-Hindi-speaking regions to enhance 'national unity'. In addition, these leaders even began to argue that the Devanagari form of numerals should be used instead of the international form. This was firmly opposed by members from South India. To solve the continuing dispute among the Assembly members, (K. M.) Munshi and N. Gopalaswami Ayyangar, a Tamil member of the Assembly, drew up detailed language provisions. These, in the words of the latter, represented a 'compromise between opinions not easily reconcilable' (Constituent Assembly Debates 1X 1966: 1319). The provisions were proposed to the Congress on 2nd September 1949 and engendered a heated discussion. It was eventually decided that they would be proposed in the Assembly by Munshi, Ayyangar, and Bhimrao Ambedkar (the Chairman of the Drafting Committee) in their personal capacities, not as an official proposal on behalf of the Drafting Committee.

The first engineering college was an outgrowth of the Ganges Canal. Named after the lieutenant governor of the North-Western Provinces who founded it in 1847, the Thomason Engineering College at Roorkee trained employees for the irrigation branch of the Public Works Department. It offered different curricula for different types of students: an engineering class for domiciled Europeans and a few Indians, an upper subordinates class to train British noncommissioned officers as construction foremen, and a lower subordinates class to train Indian surveyors. By the mid-1880s, the school has a hundred students, substantial buildings, and a reputation as an important center for the study of hydraulic engineering.

 - Lagasse, Paul, ed. (2007). ["Caste"](http://www.encyclopedia.com/topic/caste.aspx#3). *The Columbia Encyclopedia*. New York, NY: Columbia University Press. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_%28identifier%29) [978-0-231-14446-9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-231-14446-9). [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20231001133425/https://www.encyclopedia.com/social-sciences-and-law/anthropology-and-archaeology/anthropology-terms-and-concepts/caste#3) from the original on 1 October 2023. Retrieved 24 September 2012. Quote: "**caste** [Port., casta=basket], ranked groups based on heredity within rigid systems of social stratification, especially those that constitute Hindu India. Some scholars, in fact, deny that true caste systems are found outside India. The caste is a closed group whose members are severely restricted in their choice of occupation and degree of social participation. Marriage outside the caste is prohibited. Social status is determined by the caste of one's birth and may only rarely be transcended."
 - [Madan, T. N.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T._N._Madan) (2012). [*caste*](https://www.britannica.com/EBchecked/topic/98395/caste). Encyclopædia Britannica Online. [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20130130130911/http://www.britannica.com/EBchecked/topic/98395/caste) from the original on 30 January 2013. Retrieved 23 November 2025. Quote: "**caste**, any of the ranked, hereditary, endogamous social groups, often linked with occupation, that together constitute traditional societies in South Asia, particularly among Hindus in India. Although sometimes used to designate similar groups in other societies, the "caste system" is uniquely developed in Hindu societies."
 - Gupta, Dipankar (2008). ["Caste"](https://books.google.com/books?id=YMUola6pDnkC&pg=PA246). In Schaefer, Richard T. (ed.). *Encyclopedia of Race, Ethnicity, and Society*. Thousand Oaks: SAGE. pp. 246–250. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_%28identifier%29) [978-1-4129-2694-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-1-4129-2694-2). Retrieved 5 August 2012. Quote: "**Caste**: What makes Indian society unique is the phenomenon of caste. Economic, religious, and linguistic differentiations, even race-based discrimination, are known elsewhere, but nowhere else does one see caste but in India."
 - [Béteille 2002](#CITEREFBéteille2002), pp. 136–137. Quote: "**Caste**: Caste has been described as the fundamental social institution of India. Sometimes the term is used metaphorically to refer to rigid social distinctions or extreme social exclusiveness wherever found, and some authorities have used the term 'colour-caste system' to describe the stratification based on race in the United States and elsewhere. But it is among the Hindus in India that we find the system in its most fully developed form although analogous forms exist among Muslims, Christians. Sikhs and other religious groups in South Asia. It is an ancient institution, having existed for at least 2,000 years among the Hindus who developed not only elaborate caste practices hut also a complex theory to explain and justify those practices (Dumont 1970). The theory has now lost much of its force although many of the practices continue."
 - Mitchell, Geoffrey Duncan (2006). ["Castes (part of SOCIAL STRATIFICATION)"](https://books.google.com/books?id=-a4vvdBrSVgC&pg=PA194). *A New Dictionary of the Social Sciences*. New Brunswick, NJ: Aldine Transaction Publishers. pp. 194–195. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_%28identifier%29) [978-0-202-30878-4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-202-30878-4). Retrieved 10 August 2012. Quote:"**Castes** A pure caste system is rooted in the religious order and may be thought of as a hierarchy of hereditary, endogamous, occupational groups with positions fixed and mobility barred by ritual distances between each caste. Empirically, the classical Hindu system of India approximated most closely to pure caste. The system existed for some 3,000 years and continues today despite many attempts to get rid of some of its restrictions. It is essentially connected with Hinduism."
 - ["caste, n."](http://www.oed.com/view/Entry/28546). *Oxford English Dictionary, Second edition; online version June 2012*. Oxford, UK: Oxford University Press. 1989. [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20150924123226/http://www.oed.com/view/Entry/28546) from the original on 24 September 2015. Retrieved 5 August 2019. Quote: "**caste, n.** 2a. spec. One of the several hereditary classes into which society in India has from time immemorial been divided;... *This is now the leading sense, which influences all others*."

Mayer's (1960: 219) observation for central India would not be inaccurate for most communities in the subcontinent: "A man's tie with his sister is accounted very close. The two have grown up together, at an age when there is no distinction made between the sexes. And later, when the sister marries, the brother is seen as her main protector, for when her father has died to whom else can she turn if there is trouble in her conjugal household.(Adrian C. Mayer, *Caste and kinship in Central India* (1960)" The parental home, and after the parents' death the brother's home, often offers the only possibility of temporary or longer-term support in case of divorce, desertion, and even widowhood, especially for a woman without adult sons. Her dependence on this support is directly related to economic and social vulnerability.

Quote: Rakhi and its local performances in Kishan Garhi were part of a festival in which connections between out-marrying sisters and village-resident brothers were affirmed. In the "traditional" form of this rite, according to Marriott, sisters exchanged with their brothers to ensure their ability to have recourse—at a crisis, or during childbearing—to their natal village and their relatives there even after leaving for their husband's home. For their part, brothers engaging in these exchanges affirmed the otherwise hard-to-discern moral solidarity of the natal family, even after their sister's marriage.

[Ifrah 2000](#CITEREFIfrah2000), p. 346: "The measure of the genius of Indian civilisation, to which we owe our modern (number) system, is all the greater in that it was the only one in all history to have achieved this triumph. Some cultures succeeded, earlier than the Indian, in discovering one or at best two of the characteristics of this intellectual feat. But none of them managed to bring together into a complete and coherent system the necessary and sufficient conditions for a number-system with the same potential as our own."

1. [Bourbaki 1998](#CITEREFBourbaki1998), p. 49: Modern arithmetic was known during medieval times as "Modus Indorum" or method of the Indians. [Leonardo of Pisa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonardo_of_Pisa) wrote that compared to method of the Indians all other methods is a mistake. This method of the Indians is none other than our very simple arithmetic of addition, subtraction, multiplication and division. Rules for these four simple procedures was first written down by [Brahmagupta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahmagupta) during the 7th century AD. "On this point, the Hindus are already conscious of the interpretation that negative numbers must have in certain cases (a debt in a commercial problem, for instance). In the following centuries, as there is a diffusion into the West (by intermediary of the Arabs) of the methods and results of Greek and Hindu mathematics, one becomes more used to the handling of these numbers, and one begins to have other "representation" for them which are geometric or dynamic."
1. "algebra" 2007. [*Britannica Concise Encyclopedia*](https://www.britannica.com/ebc/article-231064) [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20070929134632/http://www.britannica.com/ebc/article-231064) 29 September 2007 at the [Wayback Machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayback_Machine). Encyclopædia Britannica Online. 16 May 2007. Quote: "A full-fledged decimal, positional system certainly existed in India by the 9th century (AD), yet many of its central ideas had been transmitted well before that time to China and the Islamic world. Indian arithmetic, moreover, developed consistent and correct rules for operating with positive and negative numbers and for treating zero like any other number, even in problematic contexts such as division. Several hundred years passed before European mathematicians fully integrated such ideas into the developing discipline of algebra."
1. [Pingree 2003](#CITEREFPingree2003), p. 45 Quote: "Geometry, and its branch trigonometry, was the mathematics Indian astronomers used most frequently. Greek mathematicians used the full chord and never imagined the half chord that we use today. Half chord was first used by Aryabhata which made trigonometry much more simple. In fact, the Indian astronomers in the third or fourth century, using a pre-Ptolemaic Greek table of chords, produced tables of sines and versines, from which it was trivial to derive cosines. This new system of trigonometry, produced in India, was transmitted to the Arabs in the late eighth century and by them, in an expanded form, to the Latin West and the Byzantine East in the twelfth century."
1. [Bourbaki 1998](#CITEREFBourbaki1998), p. 126: "As for trigonometry, it is disdained by geometers and abandoned to surveyors and astronomers; it is these latter ([Aristarchus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristarchus_of_Samos), [Hipparchus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hipparchus), [Ptolemy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptolemy)) who establish the fundamental relations between the sides and angles of a right angled triangle (plane or spherical) and draw up the first tables (they consist of tables giving the *chord* of the arc cut out by an angle {\displaystyle \theta <\pi } on a circle of radius *r*, in other words the number {\displaystyle 2r\sin \left(\theta /2\right)}; the introduction of the sine, more easily handled, is due to Hindu mathematicians of the Middle Ages)."

It was his insight into algebraical formulae, transformation of infinite series,... that was most amazing. On this side most certainly I have never met his equal, and I can compare him only with [Euler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonhard_Euler) and [Jacobi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Gustav_Jacob_Jacobi). He worked, far more than the majority of modern mathematicians, by induction from numerical examples.... But with his memory, his patience, and his power of calculation, he combined a power of generalisation, a feeling for form, and a capacity for rapid modification of his hypotheses, that were often really startling, and made him, in his own peculiar field, without a rival in his day.

Chapatis are made from finely milled whole-wheat flour, called chapati flour or atta, and water. The dough is rolled into thin rounds which vary in size from region to region and then cooked without fat or oil on a slightly curved griddle called a tava.

(eds.). *Yeast Biotechnology: Diversity and Applications*. Springer. p. 180. Idli is an acid-leavened and steamed cake made by bacterial fermentation of a thick batter made from coarsely ground rice and dehulled black gram. Idli cakes are soft, moist and spongy, have desirable sour flavour, and is eaten as breakfast in South India. Dosa batter is very similar to idli batter, except that both the rice and black gram are finely grounded. The batter is thinner than that of idli and is fried as a thin, crisp pancake and eaten directly in South India.
1. Jhala, Angma Day (2015). [*Royal Patronage, Power and Aesthetics in Princely India*](https://books.google.com/books?id=WGpECgAAQBAJ&pg=PA70). Routledge. p. 70. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_%28identifier%29) [978-1-317-31657-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-1-317-31657-2). With the ascent of the Mughal Empire in sixteenth-century India, Turkic, Persian and Afghan traditions of dress, 'architecture and cuisine' were adopted by non-Muslim indigenous elites in South Asia. In this manner, Central Asian cooking merged with older traditions within the subcontinent, to create such signature dishes as biryani (a fusion of the Persian pilau and the spice-laden dishes of Hindustan), and the Kashmiri meat stew of Rogan Josh. It not only generated new dishes and entire cuisines, but also fostered novel modes of eating. Such newer trends included the consumption of Persian condiments, which relied heavily on almonds, pastries and quince jams, alongside Indian achars made from sweet limes, green vegetables and curds as side relishes during Mughlai meals.
